<p>The Registry Book contains births, marriages and deaths, and in many instances all of the entries for one family have been recorded together.  Also included are lists of Loyalist settlers who have drawn town lots in Guysborough, 3 August 1790; front lots at the head of Chedabucto Bay, 2 June 1785; and in other Divisions of Towns and Townships.  Three other returns of particular interest are:  People from the late Sixtieth Regiment; A Return of the Names and Number of the Lots laid out [for] the Officers and Privates of Lord Montague's Regiment, commonly called the Duke of Cumberlands, February 1785; and A Return of Lots of Land laid out on the west side of the Gutt of Canso in the Township of Manchester to the Loyalists from St. Augustine, Florida, October 1784.</p>
<hr>

<p>A volume of documents and unsigned letters written from Halifax, perhaps by Governor Parr, or by his Secretary, to officials of the British government in London. The major portion of the correspondence deals with the settling of the Loyalists. One letter to Lord Dorchester in February 1789 concerns the amount of land to be allotted to the 84th regiment (Royal Highland Emigrants). The last page on the reel is a list of Supreme Court Petit Jurors for the Easter Term, 1797.</p>
<hr>

<p>This volume of papers contains a wealth of material pertaining to the early settlement of the St. John River. The records begin well before the American Revolution and a number of the inhabitants listed were pre-Loyalist settlers who sided with the Rebels during the Revolution. Each person's situation is described in detail, giving the number of children, length of time on the land, number of acres and type of house. A separate list of Rebel sympathizers and a list of French inhabitants are included. Much of the reel is taken up with Gilfred Studholme's accounts, which include payments to labourers; bills and receipts for money and goods, in particular lumber for houses which he distributed; and lists of Loyalists with the assistance they received.  Other important records include:  a register of lots in the Town of Parr organized by street, giving the lot number and its dimensions (unfortunately, almost completely illegible on the microfilm); a warrant of survey for land to be distributed to the 2nd Battalion of the New Jersey Volunteers with a Return of the name, rank, number of acres and number in each family to be settled on Block # 2 on the River St. John - the Return is dated 8 November 1783; lists of persons located on the Town Plot at St. Andrews, in Wentworth's Plantation on Oak Point Bay, on farm lots at Passamaquoddy, and on 50-acre lots on the St. Croix River;  a list  of  settlers  on  lands  granted on the River St. John; an assessment of the inhabitants of the Town and Parish of Campo Bello; and a long list of persons who pay a Quit Rent of one farthing per year for a lot.</p>
<hr>

<p>The Court proceedings recorded on this microfilm reel were held at Halifax and include the judgement books, and the Crown causes for various terms from 1784 to 1804.  The defendants were arraigned on many different charges and, if found guilty on the most serious, the sentence was either death or branding.  Charges included murder, rape, burglary, grand larceny, receiving stolen goods, editious words, riotous assault, and other crimes.  The verdict and the nature of the punishment were recorded, including the date of execution. Broad subject matter is crime and justice.</p>

<hr>

<p>Includes information on stockholders who have purchased <em>Funds in the Province of Nova Scotia</em>. The stock transaction records are detailed, and frequently include the date and place of residence of the stockholder. The majority of the stockholders live in Halifax, but other centres are mentioned, including: Annapolis, Lunenburg, Shelburne, London, Glasgow and Portsmouth.</p>
<hr>

<p>This microfilm reel contains "Estimates of the Charge of Maintaining and Supporting the Civil Establishment of His Majesty's Colony of Nova Scotia for the year . . ."  There is a record of the estimates for each year, with a few exceptions.  Salaries and allowances are listed in detail.  A few examples are as follows:   Lieutenant Governor, 1000; Chief Justice, 850; Surveyor General of Lands, 150; Bishop, 700; Superintendent of the Fishery, 200; Governor of Kings College in Nova Scotia for the salaries of the professors and other expenses, 1000.  After 1820, expenses for Cape Breton appear in the lists.  The documents are arranged chronologically.</p>
<hr>

<p>Returns of taxes collected in several Counties and Townships of the Province.  A few lists contain personal names and the amount of  money received from each person. Other lists are simply a report of taxes assessed and/or collected.  Included are:   Import, Excise and Poll Tax for the District of Hants; Assessment and Capitation Tax for the County of Cumberland; Capitation Tax for the Township of Shelburne; Import, Excise Tax and Capitation Tax for the Liverpool District of Queens County; and other lists.  The last document is a report from the Commissioners for Superintending the Light Houses throughout the Province of Nova Scotia, concerning the cost of repairs to the light house at Brier Island, 18 August 1821.</p>
<hr>

<p>While the bulk of the collection consists of a <strong>listing of records</strong> from the Prizes and Instance divisions of the Court of Vice Admiralty at Halifax, Nova Scotia; other listings of records are included from the Court of Escheats and Forfeitures, Court of Error, and the Court of the Governor in Council for the Sale of Intestate Estates.  Topics relate to maritime law between the period after the American Revolution and the end of the War of 1812, particularly privateering; justice and property.</p>

<p><strong>Arrangement and Detailed Content:</strong></p>

<p>The microfilm reel begins with “A List of All Ships and Vessels for which Letters of Marque have been issued out of the Vice-Admiralty Court at Halifax since the Commencement of Hostilities 1803 to the 1st June 1814.” This document is followed by, “A List of Causes which came before the Court of Vice-Admiralty, Instance Court, 1784-1802”; “Lists of Prize Causes, 1793-1794, 1797-1801, 1803-1815”; “Recaptures, 1812-1815”; “Appeal Causes, 1812-1813”; “Droits, 1812”; “Instance Court, 1811-1817”; “Letters of Marque, 1804-1815”; and “Causes, 1818-1857, 1860-1867”.  Additional information in the documents includes ship aliases, listing of captured cargoes (examples of types of cargo included oatmeal, kegs of powder, flour, tobacco, pitch, timber, rice, beef or sometimes a general notation was made: “Sundry goods seized at Halifax”), petitions of agents of captors of ships, cases (e.g. “Healy vs. Baker”), and other court documentation such as despatches. Types of documents listed include prize causes, instance causes, droits, recaptures, appeal causes, condemnations jure corone (by right of the Crown), and letters of marque.  Subjects covered in the first section of documents include ship/ vessel types, privateers, captures, ship seizures, and letters of marque.</p>

<p>The list of documents which originated from the Court of Escheats and Forfeitures, Court of Error, and the Court of the Governor in Council for the Sale of Intestate Estates are filmed from page 82 onward.  Documents are ordered as follows:  List of Interstate Estates, 1763-1842; Court of Escheats and Forfeitures - Inquisitions, 1760-1842; Escheats, Island of Cape Breton, 1817-1819; Escheats, 1781-1785; Petitions for Escheats, 1780-1837; and lastly, the Causes that came before the Court of Error, 1765-1836.</p>

<hr>

<p>A volume of documents dealing with many issues that came before the civic administration of the town.  Included are numerous memorials from government appointees, merchants, tradesmen, ship captains and private citizens; accounts of the Commissioners of Public Markets, Streets, etc.; drafts of bills; reports from committees; minutes of a Special Session of the Court of Quarter Sessions of the Peace; lists of persons committed; letters; and many petitions from groups and individuals.</p>
<hr>

<p>The Wilmot Township Book contains, among other records, registers for births, marriages and deaths for 1749-1894.  Complete families are listed together with the parents' names followed by the names and birth dates of their children.  Death dates are recorded in some instances.  Other records include:  Minutes of the Annapolis Court of General Sessions of the Peace for Wilmot Township ordering the performance of Statute Labour, 1839 and 1842; and Ear Marks for 1847-1893 and 1786-1875 with a drawing and a written description of the mark, as well as the owner's name and the date.</p>
<hr>

<p>A Minute Book of Town meetings. Much of the business concerned the accounts of the Overseers of the Poor and the tax that was levied to provide the funds for their work. Persons receiving relief are named.  Other business concerned the care of the church property and the graveyard, and the establishing of school districts.  Trustees for each school are named.</p>
<hr>

<p>The  Supreme Court  Minute  Book  for  26 October 1813 -22 February 1814 records in detail the cases which came before the Court during that period.  The names of judges, lawyers, jurors, plaintiffs and defendants are recorded, together with court orders, motions, judgements, and all other details of the proceedings.</p>
<hr>

<p>A small but important volume containing lists of land grantees in Nova Scotia. The date, the number of acres, and the District in which the land was granted is given for each person named. Districts are mentioned which later became part of the Province of New Brunswick in 1784. The names appear in a rough chronological order.</p>
<hr>

<p>A volume of land grants for the Township of Preston containing a list of proprietors, the proportion of land granted to each person, and the date of the grant.  The reel contains the details of the original survey for each of the town lots in Preston.  A list of proprietors for a tract of land granted to Thomas Young and another list for land granted to Patrick Byrns are both included in the volume.</p>
<hr>

<p><strong>War Office 65, Volume 164</strong>. A printed list of general and staff officers on the establishment in America. Included are staff officers, officers of foot regiments, artillery officers, officers of dragoons, general hospital staff, German officers, and officers of Provincial Regiments. The foot regiments are arranged in order by regimental number and the officers are listed by rank within their regiment. The information includes: rank, name, date of rank in the regiment, and date of rank in the army. The 84th Regiment or Royal Highland Emigrants is found among the British regiments. The lists of the officers of Provincial Regiments are on pages 61-84 where will be found also the dates of their joining the regiment. The lists differ considerably from the lists in Volume 165, the next volume on the reel, and from those in Treasury 64/23, both of which are available in the Loyalist Collection.</p>

<p><strong>War Office 65, Volume 165. British American Half Pay Lists (Provincial Regiments)</strong>. In 1783, General Sir Guy Carleton directed that a return be made of the names, services, and ages, up to that time, of the officers belonging to Provincial Regiments, as well as officers of the British-American Staff and others attached to that part of the service. The return was completed by Ward Chipman, Deputy Muster Master-General of Provincial Regiments from 1777-1783, and forwarded to the War Office. The Return is dated at Saint John, New Brunswick, 12 June 1822. The lists in Volume 165 are hand written, but otherwise they are similar in format to pages 61-85 in Volume 164, yet contain information not included in Volume 164 namely: age, country of origin, and lists of seconded officers also not in Treasury 64/23. Information given for the seconded officers includes: regiment they belong to, rank, name, country [of origin], age, British regimental service, and Provincial regimental service.</p>

<p><strong>War Office 65/166. Officers of British-American Forces Placed on Half Pay and Allowances at the Conclusion of the American War, 1783</strong>. This volume begins with a lengthy alphabetical index of surnames containing the page references to the half pay status of each officer. The information is arranged by regiment. As the lists were corrected to 1832, and many of the officers had died by that date, a wealth of information can be gleaned from the dates of death which are often recorded, or from the last date when half pay or an allowance was paid. The lists contain the names of Canadian and West Indian Corps not found in any other lists.</p>
<hr>

<p>The Papers which were selected for microfilming include: returns of persons; lists of disbanded soldiers and other Loyalists; memorials; drafts of grants; petitions and warrants.  Of particular significance are the following: a List of the British Legion and other Loyalists with the number of their families at Guysborough;  returns of Westchester Loyalists who have drawn land on the North and South sides of the Cobequid Road and at Ramsheg; a return of Loyalists from South Carolina who settled near Windsor; a return of Loyalists who went from New York to Great Britain and have not claimed their land in Nova Scotia; and a Draft of a Grant to George Sutherland containing two hundred and forty names, with the number of acres assigned to each person, in Clements, Annapolis County.  Locations which are mentioned in the records are: Guysborough, Ramsheg, Sissibou River, St. Mary's Bay, Cobequid Road, Dartmouth and Windsor. The Petition of Black Pioneers for land was not microfilmed.</p>
<hr>

<p>Two exceedingly rich volumes of information regarding the settlement of the Loyalists, and other records from the same time period, are contained on one reel of microfilm.  Included among the documents are memorials; petitions for land; descriptions of land grants; schedules; lists of names; estimates; certificates; returns of location; certificates of survey; accounts of expenses incurred; lists of lands forfeited; abstracts of money expended; reports; accounts of supplies delivered to Loyalists, e.g. shingles and boards; commissions; memoranda; orders; returns; bonds and minutes of meetings.  There are 174 documents in the first volume and 160 in the second.</p>
<hr>

<p>All areas of Nova Scotia are represented in these documents. Of particular significance are several volumes of militia commissions which are arranged by county and include the date of each commission.  Other records include : proclamations; instructions; appointments; commissions; native/indigenous treaties; military orders; declarations; certificates; leaves of absence; marriage licenses, (frequently recorded on the same page with a totally different document); orders; licenses to teach; pardons; oaths of allegiance; notices of land forfeiture and accounts.</p>

<p>Organisation:</p>

<p>Vol 163 Commission Book, 1749-1759</p>
<p>Vol 164 Commission Book, 1749-1766</p>
<p>Vol 165 Commission Book, 1759-1766</p>
<p>Vol 166 Treasury Warrants, 1766-1768</p>
<p>Vol 166A Treasury Warrants, 1766-1768</p>
<p>Vol 167 Commission Book, 1766-1768</p>
<p>Vol 168 Commission Book, 1768-1781</p>
<p>Vol 169 Commission Book, 1781-1783</p>
<p>Vol 170 Orderly Book, 1768-1792</p>
<p>Vol 171 Commission Book, 1793-1796</p>
<p>Vol 172 Commission Book, 1783-1809</p>
<p>Vol 173 Commission Book, 1810-1821</p>
<p>Vol 174 Commission Book, 1821-1835</p>

<hr>

<p>The documents detail the financial support given to the Jamaica Maroons mostly pertaining to their last year in Nova Scotia and the dispensation of their property upon leaving Nova Scotia in 1800.  The Records contain a volume of accounts and receipts for articles supplied and services rendered to the Maroons. Many records include detailed lists of food, clothing and other necessities with the amount charged for each item.  The accounts are arranged by date from 31 December 1798 to 8 May 1804.  Topics for research include black history, emigration, finances, business and commerce, health and medicine, Nova Scotia (Halifax, Preston, Boydville or Sackville area today), and Jamaica.  In providing information about the support given to the Maroons at the end of their stay in Nova Scotia, the documents also show some of the locals who profitted, including a few women.</p>

<p>Additional information can be gleaned from the documents: lists of supplies can include prices and supplier; service providers often contain the number of days or hours worked, wage expected and specifics of work. The documents, arranged chronologically, include the following matter:</p>

<p>1798</p>

<p>One document indicating the supplies provided by John Lawlor for beef, sugar and fish for the use of the maroons.</p>

<p>1800</p>

<p>1. Supplies - hay, medicines, foodstuffs (flour, indian meal, cracked corn, pollard)</p>
<p>2. Services - blacksmith, wheelwright, house rent, chaplain, Doctor Michael Head, labourer, building construction, overseer of labourers, assistant superintendant, surveyor of land, hauling baggage with oxeteam, horse hires to Boydville and Beaver Bank, and lawyer - Richard Uniacke for advice concerning lands at Dartmouth, right of road at Beaver Bank, care of maroon Smith, payment to sheriff for services of writ for the case of Smith versus Tinker, and drawing four deeds to establish the bounds of maroon lands</p>

<p>1801</p>

<p>1. Services - arbitrators concerning partition lines of property, overseer of labourers, taking charge of property at Boydville such as securing windows and preserving buildings from August 1800 to June 1801, mason work for large house bought by Mr. Wallace for the accomodation of the maroons, advertising maroon land for sale, surveying maroon lands and subdividing into lots, superintendant of maroons from January to October, labour for the same time period, and lawyer - writing 41 deeds (Wentworth to purchasers of maroon property), drawing arbitration bonds respecting boundaries, and advice</p>
<p>2. Supplies - sadlery, foodstuffs - flour, indian meal, cracked corn, pollard from December 1800 to August 1801</p>

<p>1802 (14 January)</p>

<p>One document concerning services provided by John Howe in advertising maroon land for sale at Preston, Windsor Road and Cole Harbour.</p>

<p>1803 (30 April)</p>

<p>One document concerning the service of the Hon. Benjamin Wentworth for registering deeds from 1799 to 1803; among other services listed within - Cochran's release.</p>

<p>1804 (8 May)</p>

<p>One document concerning the services of Theophilus Chamberlain from January 1801 to 1804 for adjusting, arranging and making out all maroon accounts since 1800 to the final close, and making final settlement.</p>

<hr>

<p>The Registers and Indexes in NAC Series RG 7, G 1 serve to identify the despatches and must be used in conjunction with the shelf list in order to locate specific despatches.  Each register contains an index.  The index serves only the incoming despatches, although, in several cases, outgoing despatches are recorded in the registers as well.  The shelf list identifies the microfilm reels in which the despatches appear.</p>
<hr>

<p>The arrangement of the correspondence files in this series is chronological. Each item was assigned a file number in the order of its receipt, which was then entered in a register.  The contemporary registers and indexes serve to identify the correspondence received and must be used in conjunction with the shelf list in order to locate specific files.  The shelf list identifies the microfilm reels on which the correspondence files will appear.</p>
<hr>

<p>The documents are organized by volume into three groups. They include Upper Canada Supplemental, documents relating to Upper Canada that were acquired after the calendar and binding of RG 7, G 1 had been completed (volume 1); miscellaneous papers relating to Upper and Lower Canada which were found in various locations in the "G" Series (volume 2); and Despatches to the Lieutenant Governor, documents which were originally part of "S" Series, including those of the Civil and Provincial Secretary of Lower Canada and Quebec (volumes 3-9 inclusive).</p>
<hr>

<p>The early records contain a considerable body of correspondence with numerous officials and other individuals.  They include:  Lieutenant Governors; the Military Secretary; Sir William Johnson; Sir John Johnson; Guy Johnson; Colonel William Claus; Lieutenant Colonel D.C. Napier; and others.  In addition to letters and copies of letters, there are petitions; instructions; reports; returns; minutes and memoranda of Indian Councils; journals of activities; census records; requisitions; commissions; general orders; claims; accounts; documents relating to land; records; and other administrative documents.</p>
<hr>

<p>The <u>General Index of Grants from 1730</u><u>-</u><u>1937</u>, inclusive, is arranged alphabetically by the name of the grantee.  Other information includes:  year of the grant, a grant number in some cases, area in acres, district, county, book and page number.  Following each letter of the alphabet, there is a supplementary list of grants entitled, <u>General Index to Grants from 1938 to </u><u>1976</u>.  Each list contains the same kind of information as can be found in the lists for the earlier<i> </i>period.  The last microfiche contains a list of Halifax Harbour water lots.</p>
<hr>

<p>The reel contains reports from the Parish Commissioners of Highways to the Court of General Sessions of the Peace giving details of road construction within each parish.  The dates include: 1789-90, 1792-93, 1798, 1800, 1802-3, 1809-10, 1812, 1814, 1817, 1822-36, 1838, 1840-62. The reports give precise information about the route the road will take, including the names of property owners, if any, along its course.  Other information includes:  the length and width of the road, the date and place the report was filed, and the signatures of all the commissioners, the great majority of whom are Loyalists.</p>

<p><u>Arrangement</u>: No real organisation; probably written in the records when had the time.</p>

<p><strong>Place and Dates in the Records (Note: This is not the arrangement!)</strong></p>

<p>Brighton: 1832</p>
<p>Douglas: 1798* (area was Queensbury at time of recording); 1823; 1825; 1828; 1830; 1831; 1832; 1833; 1834; 1835; 1836; 1838; 1840; 1841; 1842; 1844; 1845; 1846; 1847; 1848; 1849; 1851; 1852; 1854; 1855; 1857; 1859; 1860; 1861</p>
<p>Dumfries: 1849</p>
<p>Fredericton: 1826; 1830**; 1831; 1838</p>
<p>Greenhill Settlement: 1860</p>
<p>Kingsclear: 1789; 1791; 1831; 1840; 1842; 1843; 1851; 1855; 1856; 1859; 1860; 1862</p>
<p>Madamkeswick: 1812; 1817</p>
<p>Nashwack: 1790;</p>
<p>Northampton: 1789; 1803; 1809; 1826</p>
<p>Pecagumie: 1831; 1832</p>
<p>Prince William: 1841; 1852; 1853; 1854** (includes map); 1858; 1859; 1862**</p>
<p>Queensbury: 1802; 1810; 1817; 1822; 1824; 1827; 1830; 1833; 1841; 1842; 1843; 1844; 1846; 1947; 1853; 1855; 1858</p>
<p>St. Marys : 1792; 1793; 1798; 1813; 1825; 1833; 1840; 1844; 1845; 1848; 1850; 1852; 1853; 1854**; 1856**; 1857; 1858; 1859; 1861; 1862</p>
<p>Stanley: 1862</p>
<p>Southampton: 1803; 1809; 1851</p>
<p>Wakefield: 1826; 1827; 1828; 1829; 1830; 1831</p>
<p>Woodstock: 1812; 1816; 1817; 1828</p>

<hr>

<p>A list of the more extensive sections of the film includes:   minutes of town meetings, Sackville, New Brunswick, 1762-1883;  a record of sheep ear marks for Sackville, 1768;  land settlement records for the Township of Sackville, 1786;  a record of births, with a few marriages and deaths, arranged by family, 1751-1822;  two lists of land grantees in the Township of Sackville, 1773 and 1774;  minutes of town meetings, 1851-1871;  Commissions of Oyer and Terminer, 1805 and 1821;  Record of the Proceedings in the Court of Common Pleas, 1785-1830;  and Record of Proceedings of the Court of General Quarter Sessions of the Peace, 1785-1809.</p>
<hr>

<p>The Registry records for each county in New Brunswick form the official record of land transfers, beginning after the Province was established in 1784. The Charlotte County documents are arranged chronologically by date of registration. The alphabetical designation of each book refers to all land transactions within a given chronological period, and has no relationship to the surnames of the person or persons involved.  The documents are principally, deeds, mortgages and leases of land.  The records include the names of the grantor and grantee, the witnesses, and the registrar.  The date the document was received in the registrar's office and the date of registration are both given.</p>
<hr>

<p>The index is divided by date into two sections.  The first part of the reel contains the Index for Books A-Q, 1784-1838, and Books R-Z, 1838-1854.  The second section of the reel is an Index for Books 1-22, 1854-1874.   In both sections the index is alphabetically arranged by the name of the grantor and the grantee.  The instrument, type, book, page and parish are also given.</p>
<hr>

<p>There are ten register books for the first thirty-eight years of Queens County land transactions.  They provide a rich source of information on the lives of the Loyalists who were the principal settlers of this area.  Documents include:  deeds;  mortgages;  lease agreements;  receipts; oaths;  and other records. Many of the documents contain the signatures of witnesses.  The date received, the place of registration, and the date of registration are recorded, along with the signature of the Registrar at the end of each record.  The documents are filed by date of registration.</p>
<hr>

<p>The Index is divided into two parts, i.e. grantor to grantee and grantee to grantor.  Each part is arranged alphabetically by surname, followed by the book, page, instrument reference and location.  A list of the books with their respective dates is also included.  The Index is in typescript form.</p>
<hr>

<p>Land transactions of many different kinds may be found in these Records. Included are deeds;  mortgages; lease agreements; certificates; bills of sale; bonds; powers of attorney; wills;  and many other documents.</p>


<hr>

<p>The Sunbury County Consolidated Index is divided into two parts:  <strong>Section 1</strong>, 1786-1876, and <strong>Section 2</strong>, 1876-1910.   Each section is subdivided alphabetically into two parts. The Index contains the following information:   name of grantor and grantee, instrument, book, page, and the location of the land.   The date of each land transaction is not included. The list of abbreviations used in the index has been microfilmed at the beginning of each reel.</p>
<hr>

<p>The Kings County Registry Books contain the surnames of all grantors and grantees for a given period of time.  The designations A-1 to T-1 refer to an arbitrary chronological organization of the documents and do not represent any correlation with the beginning letter of the surnames.  Each Registry Book contains an alphabetical index giving the full name of both the grantor and the grantee, as well as the page reference to the document.  In a few volumes there is a second index by grantor only.</p>
<hr>

<p>The Kings County Index Books are arranged by the names of both the grantor and the grantee on adjoining pages, or as closely as each alphabetical sequence will permit.  The description of the document, i.e. deed, mortgage, etc. is given, along with the name of the parish where the land is located, and the volume and page in the Registry Books where the full text of the document can be found.</p>
<hr>

<p>Deeds and mortgages make up the vast majority of land transactions in the early Westmorland County records. However, other documents can be found including: memorial of a judgement;  assignment of mortgage; deed of partition agreement; release of mortgage;  power of attorney;  last will and testament; and others.</p>

<hr>

<p>The Index is divided into two sections: <strong>Books A-K</strong>, 1785-1848, and <strong>Books L-Z</strong>, 1848-1858. It contains the following information: book letter and number designation, date, grantor, grantee, folio, description, and location.</p>


<hr>

<p>The reel includes:   registers of baptisms, 1787-1961;  marriages, 1787-1960;  and burials, 1787-1961.   Also included are minutes of vestry meetings, 1786-1887, including records of St. John's Chapel of Ease, Chamcook;  minutes of the Deanery of St. Andrews, 1847-1900, which include minutes of the Society for the Propagation of Christian Knowledge, 1815-1831;  and a listing of notable events pertaining to the Parish of St. Stephen, 1786-1925.</p>
<hr>

<p>An index, alphabetically arranged by name of grantor and grantee, provides access to the Registry Books.  The reference includes the register volume and page number where the document can be found;  a description of the document, i.e. power of attorney, deed, mortgage etc.;  as well as the location of the land in question.</p>
<hr>

<p>The St. John County Index Books are arranged by date as follows: 1784-1823, 1823-1841, and 1841-1854. Each book is alphabetically arranged by the name of the grantor and the grantee.  The entries include the registry book letter and number references,  the page number, and the description of the document. The first part of Book I contains the Names of Persons who drew Lots in the City of Saint John, arranged by street and lot number.  This list is followed by an Alphabetical List of Lots Granted in Saint John, N.B.  The third list is A List of Lots Granted in Carleton.  The fourth, and all subsequent lists, comprise an index by name for St. John County Registry Records.</p>
<hr>

<p>The St. John Registry Books were arranged with each volume representing a specific period of time.  The registrar assigned each volume a letter, a number, and a series designation until the alphabet was complete in 1823.  At that time, a second number and series designation was begun.  The records consist of the usual deeds, mortgages, and other land transfer documents.  The registry books do not contain any type of internal index to guide the researcher.</p>
<hr>

<p>The York County Registry Records form a very large and valuable collection of early New Brunswick land transactions.  As Carleton County was not set off as a separate county from York until 1831, researchers should consult the York County records for early deeds, mortgages, and land sales of the upper St. John River Valley.</p>
<hr>

<p>The index reel is divided by date into three sections: 1786-1849, 1849-1873, and 1873-1885. Each section contains a complete alphabetical sequence of names by both grantor and grantee. Volume, number and page references follow each name.</p>

<hr>

<p>The reel contains: registers of baptisms, marriages and burials, 1794-1848; births, marriages, and adult baptisms, 1846-1907; membership rolls of the St. Stephen circuit, 1840-1943; trustees minutes, 1869-1943;  building committee minutes, 1851-1964; and quarterly meetings of the official board, 1879-1919.</p>
<hr>

<p>The collection was microfilmed in two parts, each with its own reel. The first reel contains the earlier records of the parish. These include: registers of baptisms, marriages and burials, 1792-1879; confirmation lists, 1830-1954; minutes of the Niagara District Committee of the Society for Promotion of Christian Knowledge, 1826-1830; and vestry minutes of St. Mark's Church, 1842-1898.</p>

<p>The second reel contains records relating to the role of the parish in the community. Included are: Fort George Garrison Registry, 1821-1849; registers of Rev. Thomas Green, Rector of Niagara, 1826-1839; register of marriages and baptisms, Queenston, Upper Canada, 1821-1826; minutes and reports of St. Mark's Womens' Auxilliary, 1901-1924;  and minutes, reports and attendance of 'Willing Workers' of St. Mark's Church, 1896-1937.</p>
<hr>

<p>The collection was microfilmed in two parts, each with its own reel. The first reel contains later registers and several volumes of miscellaneous records. These include:</p>
<ul>
<li>The Confession of Faith of the Church of Scotland for the Elders Lists, 1830 and 1879</li>
<li>A catalogue of the church library, 1833</li>
<li>Records of library loans, 1833-1861</li>
<li>Record of electric lights, 1893-1904</li>
<li>Marriage registers, 1905-1967</li>
<li>Minutes of congregational meetings, 1910-1930</li>
</ul>
<p>The second reel contains the earlier registers and records of the congregation which were released after microfilming had begun. Included are:</p>
<ul>
<li>Minutes of congregational meetings; lists of trustees and pew holders; financial statements; register of Christians at Newark (including  birth date information, parentage, abodes, occupations, and the names of the heads of household), 1792-1795, 1801-1802, 1809, 1814; and registers, 1794-1909</li>
<li>A register of baptisms, marriages and burials, 1830</li>
<li>Register of St. Andrew's Sabbath School, 1856-1884</li>
<li>Minute book, Niagara Auxilliary Bible Society, 1851-1923</li>
<li>Minute book, Niagara Branch [Canadian] Bible Society, 1924-1967</li>
</ul>
<hr>

<p>The records contain letters and annual and quarterly reports from the missionaries in the field to the USPG home office in London, 1722-1860. The bulk of the communicants are from the province of Nova Scotia, and to a lesser extent, New Brunswick, Quebec, Prince Edward Island, and Newfoundland. To illustrate the types of material included, the section with the letters of John Inglis contains: letters to and from the bishops of Nova Scotia; communications from Society schoolmasters; parish and church-warden petitions and memorials; newspaper cuttings; official documents; letters to and from the lieutenant-governors of Nova Scotia and Prince Edward Island, and other colonial and British officials such as the archdeacons and the Bishop of London. Wallace Brown, in the guide to this collection states: "the scope of the records is much wider than religion, partly because of the central role the church played in people’s lives, making the content a treasure trove for the study of local history", particularly social history, including primary and Sunday school education, the American Loyalists, blacks, natives (Indians), immigration and emigration. They also detail relationships between Anglicans and other religious orders, including statistics, and covers subjects such as politics; economics; trade; education (e.g. King’s College); and charitable endeavors (charity) due to fire, shipwrecks, poverty, disease. As well, the records deal with much about the everyday, such as weather, salary, travel conditions, illness, and personal tribulations.</p>

<p><strong>Arrangement and Detailed Contents</strong></p>

<p>The collection is organized into 16 sections (CAN/PreQue, CAN/NS/1-15), and 139 subsections called folders; thus within each section, the material has many folders and is not in a strictly chronological order as a whole. The main correspondents, their geographical locations and the years during which they sent to London their correspondence are detailed as follows:</p>

<p><strong>Reel 1:</strong> C/Can/Pre Quebec and Montreal, 1759-92; Newfoundland, 1772-87</p>
<ul><li>Main correspondents: Rev. James Balfour, Harbour Grace, 1772-83; Rev. John Doty, Montreal/Sorel, 1778-93; Rev. Walter Price, St. Johns, 1784</li></ul>

<p><strong>Reel 1:</strong> C/CAN/NS 1 (nos. 1-115) Nova Scotia, 1752-91</p>

Bernard Houseal, Halifax, 1784-91; Rev. George Panton, Shelburne, 1783-5; Rev. Peter de la Roche, Lunenburg, 1783-5; Rev. J.C. Wagner, London, 1786; Rev. William Walter, Shelburne, 1783-90; Rev. Joshua W. Weeks, Halifax/London, 1779-85; Rev. John Wiswall, Cornwallis, 1784-6</li></ul>

J.W.D. Gray, England/Amherst, 1822-4; Rev. Henry Haden, Rawdon/Sackville, 1820-39; Rev. Louis C. Jenkins, Charlottetown, 1819-54; Rev. W.B. King, Windsor, 1828-49; Rev. <ul><li>Rev. Cornelius Griffin (concerning the case of).</li></ul>

J.S. Smith, 1853-58; Rev. H.B. Swabey, Prince Edward Island, 1854-58; Rev. S.D. Gren, Musquodoboit, 1855-56; Rev. P. Tocque, 1855-57; Rev. W.G.T. Jarvis, Guysborough, 1854-58; Rev T.D.
